```json
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::CochlearImplantsCI"
  ]
}
```

### Note 1788732559846
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "During adult CI evaluation, an aided CNC word score {{c1::≤50}} %&nbsp;in the ear being considered for implantation supports cochlear implant candidacy."
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "This is an ear-specific clinical benchmark recommended by the American Cochlear Implant Alliance.<br><br>Keep it distinct from:<br>• Revised 60/60 rule → referral<br>• Medicare ≤60% sentence recognition → coverage"
    }
  ],
  "guid": "g3dcHI&n5D",
  "id": 1788732559846,
  "media": [],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::CochlearImplantsCI"
  ]
}
```

### Note 1788732559847
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "As of 2026, Medicare defines limited benefit from amplification for cochlear implantation as {{c1::≤60}}% correct on recorded {{c2::open-set sentence::word/sentence}} recognition&nbsp;in the best-aided condition."
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "AzBio is commonly used for sentence recognition testing.<br><br>Three numbers worth keeping straight:<br>• 60/60: referral → PTA ≥60 dB + unaided WRS ≤60%<br>• CNC ≤50%: ear-specific clinical candidacy benchmark<br>• Sentences ≤60%: Medicare coverage criterion"
    }
  ],
  "guid": "q`{0^Y.E(3",
  "id": 1788732559847,
  "media": [],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::CochlearImplantsCI"
  ]
}
```

### Note 1788732559848
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "A cochlear implant bypasses dysfunctional {{c1::cochlear hair-cell}} transduction&nbsp;and directly electrically stimulates the {{c2::spiral ganglion/auditory nerve}}."
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "A hearing aid amplifies acoustic energy but still depends on cochlear sensory transduction.<br><br>A CI instead converts sound into an electrical code and stimulates surviving auditory neurons.<br><br><img src=\"paste-ef844f5fca01ec587bbbb8194e9ee65ca32c1e87.jpg\">"
    }
  ],
  "guid": "eP2;03oA5?",
  "id": 1788732559848,
  "media": [
    "paste-ef844f5fca01ec587bbbb8194e9ee65ca32c1e87.jpg"
  ],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::CochlearImplantsCI"
  ]
}
```

### Note 1788732559849
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "Compared with normal acoustic hearing, cochlear implants provide relatively good {{c1::temporal}} information but limited {{c2::spectral/frequency}} resolution."
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "Limited spectral resolution helps explain why speech understanding can become excellent while pitch perception, music appreciation, and speech recognition in noise remain more difficult.<br><br>A major reason is current spread between neighboring electrodes."
    }
  ],
  "guid": "CE;{G5eY<f",
  "id": 1788732559849,
  "media": [],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::CochlearImplantsCI"
  ]
}
```

### Note 1788732559850
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "{{c1::Lateral-wall}} cochlear implant electrode arrays are generally designed to emphasize {{c2::atraumatic insertion and preservation of residual hearing}}."
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "These arrays lie along the lateral wall of the scala tympani and are generally thin and flexible.<br><br>They are particularly conceptually attractive when preservation of useful low-frequency acoustic hearing is a priority.<br><img src=\"paste-2f2992e39ecae6928514cf3cf6b5b13ee1b2e400.jpg\">"
    }
  ],
  "guid": "g;YQjHSw_&",
  "id": 1788732559850,
  "media": [
    "paste-2f2992e39ecae6928514cf3cf6b5b13ee1b2e400.jpg"
  ],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::CochlearImplantsCI"
  ]
}
```

### Note 1788732559851
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "{{c1::Perimodiolar}} cochlear implant electrode arrays position the electrodes {{c2::closer to the modiolus/spiral ganglion neurons}}."
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "Reducing electrode-neuron distance can decrease the current required for stimulation and potentially produce more spatially focused stimulation.<br><br>The array curves medially toward the modiolus rather than lying against the lateral cochlear wall.<br><img src=\"paste-2f2992e39ecae6928514cf3cf6b5b13ee1b2e400.jpg\">"
    }
  ],
  "guid": "Nz5ReF#])h",
  "id": 1788732559851,
  "media": [
    "paste-2f2992e39ecae6928514cf3cf6b5b13ee1b2e400.jpg"
  ],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::CochlearImplantsCI"
  ]
}
```

### Note 1788732559852
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "What is scalar translocation during cochlear implantation?<br>{{c1::Passage of the electrode array from the scala tympani into the scala vestibuli during insertion.}}"
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "The electrode disrupts structures separating the scalae and leaves its intended scala tympani trajectory.<br><br>Scalar translocation represents intracochlear trauma and is associated with poorer preservation of residual hearing and potentially worse speech outcomes.<br><img src=\"paste-320aed9d32429bf90d7232cd9e6235122371a52e.jpg\">"
    }
  ],
  "guid": "o6ijWmA~A?",
  "id": 1788732559852,
  "media": [
    "paste-320aed9d32429bf90d7232cd9e6235122371a52e.jpg"
  ],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::CochlearImplantsCI"
  ]
}
```

### Note 1788732559853
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "The number of functional spectral channels in a cochlear implant is less than the number of physical electrodes largely because of {{c1::current spread and overlapping neural stimulation}}."
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "Current from an electrode spreads through conductive cochlear fluid and tissue, so neighboring electrodes stimulate overlapping populations of spiral ganglion neurons.<br><br>This channel interaction limits the independent spectral information provided by additional electrodes."
    }
  ],
  "guid": "f@&WK$<8mh",
  "id": 1788732559853,
  "media": [],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::CochlearImplantsCI"
  ]
}
```

### Note 1788732559854
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "Greater cochlear implant electrode insertion depth provides greater access to {{c1::apical, lower-frequency regions of the cochlea}}, but must be balanced against {{c2::intracochlear trauma and preservation of residual hearing}}."
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "Insertion depth is a tradeoff between tonotopic cochlear coverage and atraumatic insertion.<br><br>Deeper is therefore not inherently better. Electrode length/depth should fit the patient's anatomy, hearing profile, and goals for residual-hearing preservation."
    }
  ],
  "guid": "cEV^myzkA}",
  "id": 1788732559854,
  "media": [],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::CochlearImplantsCI"
  ]
}
```

### Note 1788732559855
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "During cochlear implant mapping, the {{c1::T-level}} represents the minimum electrical stimulation producing auditory perception, while the {{c2::C-level/M-level}} represents the upper comfortable stimulation level."
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "Terminology varies by manufacturer.<br><br>The electrical dynamic range between threshold and comfortable loudness is narrow compared with normal acoustic hearing. The processor therefore compresses incoming acoustic intensities into this usable electrical range."
    }
  ],
  "guid": "PHpdl,eu.(",
  "id": 1788732559855,
  "media": [],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::CochlearImplantsCI"
  ]
}
```

### Note 1788732559856
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "Combining preserved low-frequency acoustic hearing with cochlear implant electrical stimulation in the same ear is called {{c1::electroacoustic stimulation (EAS)}}."
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "Typically:<br>• preserved low-frequency acoustic hearing → conventional acoustic amplification<br>• poorer higher-frequency hearing → electrical stimulation<br><br>Preserved acoustic hearing can provide pitch and fine spectral information that a CI represents poorly, improving sound quality, music perception, and speech perception in noise.<br><img alt=\"The World's Game -- Electronic Arts Announces Multiplatform EA SPORTS FIFA Global Expansion\" src=\"images-163e97ee36315cb26ee8a7302a84f8a053e36b0c.jpg\">"
    }
  ],
  "guid": "NLbs)I43/K",
  "id": 1788732559856,
  "media": [
    "images-163e97ee36315cb26ee8a7302a84f8a053e36b0c.jpg"
  ],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::CochlearImplantsCI"
  ]
}
```

### Note 1788732559858
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "An auditory brainstem implant (ABI) bypasses both the {{c1::cochlea and cochlear nerve}} and directly stimulates the {{c2::cochlear nucleus}}."
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "Think anatomically:<br><br>Hearing aid → requires cochlear sensory transduction + CN VIII<br>Cochlear implant → bypasses cochlear sensory transduction but requires a functional auditory nerve pathway<br>ABI → bypasses cochlea + CN VIII<br><br>The classic ABI population is patients with NF2-related bilateral vestibular schwannomas in whom functional cochlear nerve input is absent or cannot be preserved.<br><img src=\"paste-fc82bf235c9f6675638933da08b7839936ae9913.jpg\">"
    }
  ],
  "guid": "gL`_stz))=",
  "id": 1788732559858,
  "media": [
    "paste-fc82bf235c9f6675638933da08b7839936ae9913.jpg"
  ],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::CochlearImplantsCI"
  ]
}
```

### Note 1788739141986
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "Conventional hearing aids primarily improve {{c1::audibility}}, but do <b>not</b> restore normal cochlear processing"
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "A hearing aid can make acoustic information loud enough to detect, but a damaged cochlea may still encode that information poorly. This explains why some patients say, “I can hear you, but I can’t understand you.”"
    }
  ],
  "guid": "H+8c3)?df%",
  "id": 1788739141986,
  "media": [],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::AssistiveHearingDevicesHearingAids"
  ]
}
```

### Note 1788739141988
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "Outer hair cell loss causes loss of normal {{c1::cochlear compression}} and {{c1::recruitment}}, producing a narrowed {{c2::dynamic range}} for hearing."
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "The threshold for hearing rises substantially, while the uncomfortable loudness level rises much less. Therefore, simply adding the same amount of gain to every sound would make loud sounds intolerably loud."
    }
  ],
  "guid": "l<wtD-]bFm",
  "id": 1788739141988,
  "media": [],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::AssistiveHearingDevicesHearingAids"
  ]
}
```

### Note 1788739141989
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "{{c1::Wide dynamic range compression (WDRC)}} provides relatively more gain to soft sounds and progressively less gain to louder sounds"
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "WDRC attempts to fit the large dynamic range of environmental sound into the reduced usable dynamic range of a patient with SNHL.<br><br>A compression ratio of 2:1 means a 20-dB increase in input produces only a 10-dB increase in output above the compression kneepoint."
    }
  ],
  "guid": "jiaD2dd?tP",
  "id": 1788739141989,
  "media": [],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::AssistiveHearingDevicesHearingAids"
  ]
}
```

### Note 1788739141991
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "Modern hearing aids divide sound into {{c1::frequency channels}}, allowing frequency-specific {{c2::gain}} and {{c2::compression}} based on the patient's hearing loss."
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "A patient with preserved low-frequency hearing but severe high-frequency SNHL should not receive equal amplification across all frequencies. The audiogram therefore directly informs hearing-aid programming."
    }
  ],
  "guid": "r&~lS$X^<v",
  "id": 1788739141991,
  "media": [],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::AssistiveHearingDevicesHearingAids"
  ]
}
```

### Note 1788739141994
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "Poor {{c1::word recognition scores (WRS)}} suggest that simply making speech {{c2::louder}} may not produce good speech understanding."
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "Pure-tone thresholds primarily assess <b>detection/audibility</b>. WRS assesses the ability to discriminate suprathreshold speech.<br><br>Two patients with similar pure-tone audiograms can therefore derive very different benefit from amplification."
    }
  ],
  "guid": "ICq@Swvv)>",
  "id": 1788739141994,
  "media": [],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::AssistiveHearingDevicesHearingAids"
  ]
}
```

### Note 1788739141996
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "{{c1::Real-ear measurement (REM)}} uses a probe microphone near the tympanic membrane to verify the actual hearing-aid output reaching the patient's ear."
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "Programming software predicts hearing-aid output, but individual ear-canal acoustics vary.<br><br>REM allows the audiologist to compare the <b>measured output at the patient's TM</b> with a prescriptive target and adjust the device accordingly.<br><br>Think: <b>programming = prediction; REM = verification.</b>"
    }
  ],
  "guid": "Kp.({&twDn",
  "id": 1788739141996,
  "media": [],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::AssistiveHearingDevicesHearingAids"
  ]
}
```

### Note 1788739141997
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "{{c1::NAL-NL2}} is a commonly used prescriptive hearing-aid fitting algorithm that determines frequency- and input-specific amplification targets."
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "You do not need to know the algorithm.<br><br>Conceptually:<br><b>Audiogram → NAL-NL2 target → hearing-aid programming → REM verification</b><br><br>The goal is useful speech audibility while maintaining acceptable loudness."
    }
  ],
  "guid": "QP_e%RRpy=",
  "id": 1788739141997,
  "media": [],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::AssistiveHearingDevicesHearingAids"
  ]
}
```

### Note 1788739141998
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "A(n) {{c1::open-fit}} hearing aid is particularly useful for patients with {{c2::high-frequency SNHL and preserved low-frequency hearing}}."
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "The open canal allows low-frequency sound to reach the TM naturally while the hearing aid provides needed high-frequency amplification.<br><br>Benefits include reduced occlusion effect and more natural perception of the patient's own voice.<br><br>RIC devices are commonly used for this configuration.<br><img src=\"paste-cc2b6566d04a19bb1b180578a6e467e3b66d4810.jpg\">"
    }
  ],
  "guid": "B&:9NX2F_s",
  "id": 1788739141998,
  "media": [
    "paste-cc2b6566d04a19bb1b180578a6e467e3b66d4810.jpg"
  ],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::AssistiveHearingDevicesHearingAids"
  ]
}
```

### Note 1788739141999
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "As required hearing-aid gain increases, a more {{c1::closed}}&nbsp;acoustic coupling/earmold is often needed to prevent sound leakage and acoustic feedback."
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "<img src=\"paste-cc2b6566d04a19bb1b180578a6e467e3b66d4810.jpg\"><br><b>More open fitting</b><br>→ less occlusion<br>→ more natural residual hearing<br>→ more sound leakage<br><br><b>More closed fitting</b><br>→ permits greater gain<br>→ reduces feedback<br>→ greater risk of occlusion effect"
    }
  ],
  "guid": "j8S`HFW!Ri",
  "id": 1788739141999,
  "media": [
    "paste-cc2b6566d04a19bb1b180578a6e467e3b66d4810.jpg"
  ],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::AssistiveHearingDevicesHearingAids"
  ]
}
```

### Note 1788739142000
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "Hearing-aid {{c1::acoustic feedback}} occurs when amplified sound from the receiver leaks back to the microphone and is re-amplified."
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "This creates a positive feedback loop and produces the characteristic hearing-aid squeal.<br><br>Can be problematic in patients that have an open hearing aid and need alot of amplification<br><br>Greater gain and inadequate acoustic sealing increase the risk of feedback. Modern hearing aids also use electronic feedback-cancellation algorithms.<br><img src=\"paste-cc2b6566d04a19bb1b180578a6e467e3b66d4810.jpg\">"
    }
  ],
  "guid": "y.BRFkAlX$",
  "id": 1788739142000,
  "media": [
    "paste-cc2b6566d04a19bb1b180578a6e467e3b66d4810.jpg"
  ],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::AssistiveHearingDevicesHearingAids"
  ]
}
```

### Note 1788739142001
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "The major benefit of {{c1::directional microphones}} is improvement in the {{c2::signal-to-noise ratio (SNR)}} rather than simply increasing overall amplification."
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "If speech is 65 dB and background noise is 60 dB, amplifying both by 20 dB does <b>not</b> change the +5-dB SNR.<br><br>Directional microphones preferentially emphasize sound from a desired direction—often in front of the listener—relative to competing noise."
    }
  ],
  "guid": "jC.qg>vr6G",
  "id": 1788739142001,
  "media": [],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::AssistiveHearingDevicesHearingAids"
  ]
}
```

### Note 1788739142002
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "{{c1::Remote microphone systems}} improve speech understanding in noise by placing the microphone close to the talker, thereby improving the signal-to-noise ratio before the signal reaches the hearing aid."
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "Instead of trying to computationally separate speech from noise after both reach a microphone at the listener's ear, the microphone captures speech close to its source.<br><br>Particularly useful in classrooms, lectures, meetings, and other noisy environments."
    }
  ],
  "guid": "j%{W/0Fu0g",
  "id": 1788739142002,
  "media": [],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::AssistiveHearingDevicesHearingAids"
  ]
}
```

```json
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "{{c1::CROS}} is used when one ear is unaidable and the contralateral ear has {{c2::normal/near-normal}} hearing"
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "<b>CROS:</b><br>Deaf-side microphone → routes signal to good ear.<br><br>"
    }
  ],
  "guid": "j~TR=DUYo)",

  ]
}
```

### Note 1788739142004
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "Text",
      "value": "What are the major types/configurations of conventional hearing aids?<br><br>{{c1::Behind-the-ear (BTE)}}<br>{{c1::Receiver-in-canal (RIC/RITE)}}<br>{{c1::In-the-ear (ITE), including ITC/CIC}}"
    },
    {
      "name": "Extra",
      "value": "<b>BTE — behind-the-ear</b><br>Microphone, processor, and receiver are behind the ear. Sound travels through tubing to an earmold. Can provide substantial power; commonly used for severe losses and in children. Larger/more visible but durable and flexible.<br><br><b>RIC/RITE — receiver-in-canal/ear</b><br>Microphone and processor sit behind the ear. The receiver (speaker) sits in the ear canal, connected by a thin wire. Very common modern configuration and particularly well suited to open fittings and sloping high-frequency SNHL.<br><br><b>ITE — in-the-ear</b><br>Electronics are contained within a custom shell in the external ear. Smaller subtypes include ITC (in-the-canal) and CIC (completely-in-canal). More cosmetically discreet, but generally less room for battery, microphones, and other components and less capable of very high output than larger devices.<br><br><b>Key distinction:</b> BTE/RIC have components behind the ear; ITE/ITC/CIC place the device primarily or entirely within the external ear/canal.<br><br><img src=\"paste-e8b4e884ef03a232b985d6a580c9f9d44c3cc81f.jpg\"><br><img src=\"paste-adfd9af82694424e9297ba957a7e9ed51100d92b.jpg\">"
    }
  ],
  "guid": "K*)-M*W1PN",
  "id": 1788739142004,
  "media": [
    "paste-adfd9af82694424e9297ba957a7e9ed51100d92b.jpg",
    "paste-e8b4e884ef03a232b985d6a580c9f9d44c3cc81f.jpg"
  ],
  "model_id": 1524000174356,
  "tags": [
    "Crabs::Residency::Otology::AssistiveHearingDevicesHearingAids"
  ]
}
```

```json
{
```

```json
        "value": "<div>The muscle exposed deep to the sternohyoid is the thyrohyoid (inserts on the oblique line of the thyroid.<br>This improves exposure. ### Note 1687471871427
```
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "after": {
        "name": "Text",
        "value": "The {{c2::frontal (temporal)}} branch of the facial nerve travels on the deep aspect of the {{c1::superficial temporoparietal}} fascia"
      },
      "before": {
        "name": "Text",
        "value": "The {{c2::frontal (temporal)}} branch of the facial nerve travels just deep to the {{c1::temporoparietal}} fascia"
      },
      "ordinal": 0
    },
    {
      "after": {
        "name": "Extra",
        "value": "Along Pitanguy's line.<br><br>Thus if you are elevating the scalp such as in a brow lift or facelift, you need to make sure that you are deep to or superficial to this plane to protect the nerve<br><img src=\"paste-65c61312f627ad07d5e9b6ad5a124989197b4c23.jpg\">"
      },
      "before": {
        "name": "Extra",
        "value": "Along Pitanguy's line.<br><br>Thus if you are elevating the scalp such as in a borw lift or facelift, you need to make sure that you avoid this plane.<br><img src=\"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/326722296/figure/fig1/AS:654506568204289@1533057934289/Schematic-drawing-of-zygomatic-arch-area-Facial-nerve-injury-can-be-avoided-with-Gillies.png\"><img src=\"42407tn.jpg\">"
      },
      "ordinal": 1
    }
  ],
  "method": "guid",
  "new": "1687471871427",
  "old": "1687471871427",

  "tags_removed": []
```

### Note 1688219384344
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "after": {
        "name": "Extra",
        "value": "<div><div><div><div><div><div><div><div><div>The ~30 dB figure represents <strong>impedance-matching/transformer gain</strong> from the TM-to-stapes area ratio (~25 dB) + ossicular lever (~2 dB). With complete ossicular discontinuity, you don't just lose this gain—you <strong>decouple the TM from the oval window</strong>, eliminating the normal air-conduction pathway. The cochlea is then stimulated only through inefficient alternative acoustic/tissue pathways, producing a <strong>maximal conductive loss of ~50–60 dB</strong>.</div></div></div></div></div><div></div><div><div></div></div></div><div><div></div></div></div></div></div><div></div>"
      },
      "before": {
        "name": "Extra",
        "value": ""
      },
      "ordinal": 1
    }
  ],
  "method": "guid",
  "new": "1688219384344",
  "old": "1688219384344",
  "tags_added": [],
  "tags_removed": []
}
```

### Note 1688837892401
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "after": {
        "name": "Text",
        "value": "In the {{c1::extended deep plane}} face lift, inferior dissection continues to the border of the {{c2::mandible}} and {{c3::the cervical retaining}} ligaments are released."
      },
      "before": {
        "name": "Text",
        "value": "In the {{c1::extended deep plane}} face lift, inferior dissection continues to the border of the {{c2::mandible and the cervical retaining::2}} ligaments are released."
      },
      "ordinal": 0
    },
    {
      "after": {
        "name": "Extra",
        "value": "<div>Think of the <strong>cervical retaining ligaments as little fibrous tethers holding the platysma down to the deeper neck</strong>.</div>\n<div>Normally you have:</div>\n<div><strong>skin → fat → platysma → deep cervical fascia/muscles</strong></div>\n<div>The platysma isn't just freely sliding over everything underneath it. Along its <strong>lateral/posterior portion, near the anterior border of the SCM</strong>, fibrous connections run between the platysma and the deep cervical fascia:</div>\n<div><strong>Platysma</strong><br>\n↕️ <strong>cervical retaining ligaments</strong><br></div><strong>Deep cervical fascia / SCM region<br></strong><a href=\"https://drive.google.com/file/d/1jUz02Au-XnoJAzZqPOWfTNnL8pHsxpoY/view?usp=sharing\">https://drive.google.com/file/d/1jUz02Au-XnoJAzZqPOWfTNnL8pHsxpoY/view?usp=sharing</a><br><img src=\"paste-b911b32fef7cfb7ee40c6aeb5e0dc4bc149af02b.jpg\"><br><img src=\"Deep-Plane-Facelift-Figure-2a-labeled-EN-300x228.png\"><br><br><br>"
      },
      "before": {
        "name": "Extra",
        "value": "<img src=\"Deep-Plane-Facelift-Figure-2a-labeled-EN-300x228.png\">"
      },
      "ordinal": 1
    }
  ],
  "method": "guid",
  "new": "1688837892401",
  "old": "1688837892401",
  "tags_added": [],
  "tags_removed": []
}
```

### Note 1697230532666
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "after": {
        "name": "Extra",
        "value": "An itchy ear is pathognomonic.<br>Most common pathogen is aspergillus niger (80-90%). Candida albicans makes up 10%.<br>The otitis can cause TM perforations<br><div>Treatment will require antifungal drops (clotrimazole or acetic acid) and likely multiple debridements in the clinic</div>"
      },
      "before": {
        "name": "Extra",
        "value": "The above is aspergillus infection given the black conidiophores on top of fluffy white filamentous hyphae.<br><br>An itchy ear is pathognomonic.<br>Most common pathogen is aspergillus niger (80-90%). Candida albicans makes up 10%.<br><div>Treatment will require antifungal drops (clotrimazole or acetic acid) and likely multiple debridements in the clinic</div>"
      },
      "ordinal": 1
    }
  ],
  "method": "guid",
  "new": "1697230532666",
```

### Note 1698945013043
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "after": {
        "name": "Extra",
        "value": "It is a transposition flap. It is differentiated from a Dufourmental flap in that it is constructed of equal length segments of 2 120° and 2 60° angles.<br><br>Advantages: better distrubtion of tension<br>Disadvantage: forces a facial defect into an arbitrary design<br>Common Use: cheek, temple<br><br><li><strong>Type:</strong> Rhombic transposition flap\n</li>\n<li>\n<strong>Geometry:</strong> Rhombus with <strong>60° and 120° angles</strong>; all sides equal\n</li>\n<li>\n<strong>Design:</strong> Flap extends from the <strong>short diagonal</strong>; flap sides equal the sides of the defect\n</li>\n<li>\n<strong>Movement:</strong> Flap <strong>transposes/pivots</strong> into the primary defect; donor site closed primarily\n</li>\n<li>\n<strong>Tension:</strong> Greatest closure tension occurs at the <strong>donor site</strong>, approximately <strong>20° from the short diagonal across the flap base</strong>\n</li>\n<li>\n<strong>Key planning principle:</strong> Choose the flap orientation based on <strong>skin mobility and extensibility</strong> so donor-site tension falls in the most favorable direction\n</li>\n<li>\n<strong>Undermining:</strong> Wide surrounding undermining has <strong>minimal effect on wound-closure tension</strong>\n</li>\n<li>\n<strong>Advantage:</strong> Recruits adjacent similar tissue while <strong>redirecting tension away from the primary defect</strong>\n</li>\n<li>\n<strong>Pearl:</strong> Multiple Limberg flap designs are geometrically possible → choose the one with the <strong>best donor-site laxity and scar orientation</strong></li><li><strong><br></strong></li><li>Can be designed four different ways<br></li><li><img src=\"paste-b6a28b63bccf7a47c0679ee7962fef7dba1ca236.jpg\"><b></b></li>"
      },
      "before": {
        "name": "Extra",
        "value": "It is a transposition flap. It is differentiated from a Dufourmental flap in that it is constructed of equal length segments of 2 120° and 2 60° angles.<br><br>Advantages: better distrubtion of tension<br>Disadvantage: forces a facial defect into an arbitrary design<br>Common Use: cheek, temple"
      },
      "ordinal": 1
    }
  ],
  "method": "guid",
  "new": "1698945013043",
  "old": "1698945013043",
  "tags_added": [
    "Crabs::Residency::FacialPlastics::LocalFlaps::Pivotal::Rhombic"
  ],
```

```json
}
```

### Note 1699220385509
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "after": {
        "name": "Extra",
        "value": "Alexander is intense<br><img src=\"paste-95fa7400614897b4def27cb055a4bb2f51bd1198.jpg\"><br><br><h3>Right vestibular neuritis</h3>\n<div><strong>Ewald’s laws → Why does it beat LEFT?</strong></div>\n<div>The horizontal semicircular canals work as a push-pull pair. For the horizontal canal, <strong>ampullopetal endolymph flow is excitatory</strong>, and excitation produces a stronger response than inhibition.</div>\n<div>Normally, the right and left sides have balanced tonic firing.</div>\n<div>With <strong>right vestibular neuritis</strong>:</div>\n<div><strong>↓ right vestibular firing → unopposed relative LEFT-sided activity</strong></div>\n<div>Functionally, the CNS interprets this asymmetry as <strong>leftward head rotation</strong>.</div>\n<div>Ewald's principles predict the VOR associated with that apparent leftward rotation:</div>\n<div><strong>Relative LEFT excitation</strong><br>\n→ eyes driven slowly <strong>RIGHT</strong><br>\n→ corrective saccade <strong>LEFT</strong><br>\n→ <strong>LEFT-beating nystagmus</strong></div>\n<div>Remember, nystagmus is named by the <strong>fast phase</strong>.</div>\n\n<div><strong>Alexander’s law → Why does it get worse looking LEFT?</strong></div>\n<div>Alexander's law says:</div>\n<blockquote>\n<div><strong>Vestibular nystagmus becomes stronger when the patient looks in the direction of the FAST phase.</strong></div>\n</blockquote>\n<div>The fast phase in this patient is <strong>LEFT</strong>.</div>\n<div>Therefore:</div>\n<div><strong>Look LEFT → stronger left-beating nystagmus</strong><br>\n<strong>Look straight → less intense</strong><br>\n<strong>Look RIGHT → weakest</strong></div>\n<h3>Put them together</h3>\n<blockquote>\n<div><strong>Ewald explains the direction; Alexander explains the gaze dependence.</strong></div>\n</blockquote>\n<div><strong>RIGHT vestibular neuritis</strong><br>\n→ ↓ RIGHT vestibular tone<br>\n→ relative LEFT vestibular excitation<br>\n→ <strong>Ewald:</strong> apparent LEFT head rotation → slow phase RIGHT → fast phase LEFT<br>\n→ <strong>LEFT-beating nystagmus</strong><br>\n→ <strong>Alexander:</strong> looking toward the fast phase increases nystagmus<br>\n→ <strong>worse with LEFT gaze</strong></div>\n<div>So the compact rule is:</div>\n<div><strong>Weak RIGHT ear → beats LEFT → worse looking LEFT.</strong></div>"
      },
      "before": {
        "name": "Extra",
        "value": "Alexander is intense<br><img src=\"paste-95fa7400614897b4def27cb055a4bb2f51bd1198.jpg\">"
      },
      "ordinal": 1
    }
  ],
  "method": "guid",
  "new": "1699220385509",
  "old": "1699220385509",
  "tags_added": [],
  "tags_removed": []
}
```

### Note 1699696192307
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "after": {
        "name": "Text",
        "value": "Laryngeal cancer<br>The {{c1::quadrangular membrane}} resists spread of {{c2::supraglottic}} tumors to the glottis"
      },
      "before": {
        "name": "Text",
        "value": "Laryngeal cancer<br>The {{c1::quadrangular membrane}} resists spread of {{c2::supraglottic}} tumors"
      },
      "ordinal": 0
    },
    {
      "after": {
        "name": "Extra",
        "value": "<img src=\"paste-74b50ec934fa9eba221c3937159140219d418faa.jpg\"><br><div><br></div><div><br></div><div>Quadrangular Membrane (Supraglottis)</div><div><br></div><div>Located above the ventricle.</div><div><br></div><div>Attachments:</div><div><br></div><div>* Superior: lateral epiglottis</div><div>* Posterior: arytenoid cartilage</div><div><br></div><div>Free edges:</div><div><br></div><div>* Superior free edge → aryepiglottic fold</div><div>* Inferior free edge → vestibular ligament (forms the false vocal fold)</div><div><br></div><div>Function:</div><div><br></div><div>* Supports the supraglottic larynx</div><div>* Forms the framework of the false cords and aryepiglottic folds</div><div><br></div><div>Conus Elasticus (Glottis/Subglottis)</div><div><br></div><div>Located below the ventricle.</div><div><br></div><div>Attachments:</div><div><br></div><div>* Inferiorly: superior border of the cricoid cartilage</div><div>* Anteriorly: thyroid cartilage</div><div>* Posteriorly: vocal process of the arytenoid</div><div><br></div><div>Free superior edge:</div><div><br></div><div>* Vocal ligament (forms the true vocal fold)</div><div><br></div><div>Function:</div><div><br></div><div>* Supports the true vocal folds</div><div>* Forms the cricothyroid membrane anteriorly</div><div>* Provides the elastic recoil of the vocal folds</div><div></div><div><div><div><div><div></div></div></div></div></div>"
      },
      "before": {
        "name": "Extra",
        "value": "<img src=\"paste-74b50ec934fa9eba221c3937159140219d418faa.jpg\"><br><div><div><div><div><div>The quadrangular membrane is a paired fibroelastic sheet of the supraglottis that spans from the lateral arytenoid to the lateral epiglottis; its superior free edge forms the aryepiglottic fold and its inferior thickened edge becomes the vestibular ligament beneath the false vocal fold.</div></div></div></div></div><div></div><div><div><div><div><div></div></div></div></div></div>"
      },
      "ordinal": 1
    }
  ],
  "method": "guid",
  "new": "1699696192307",
  "old": "1699696192307",
  "tags_added": [],
  "tags_removed": []
}
```

```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "after": {
        "name": "Extra",
```
```json
        "value": "Documentation must support a true, separate E/M beyond the procedure itself.<br>You can only document one CPT coe during an office visit so from a financial perspective you should document the one that bills the highest<br><br>ex<br><h3><strong>Clinic visit + flexible laryngoscopy</strong></h3>\n<ul>\n<li>\n<div>Patient presents with new hoarseness</div>\n</li>\n<li>\n<div>You:</div>\n<ul>\n<li>\n<div>Take a history</div>\n</li>\n<li>\n<div>Perform head &amp; neck exam</div>\n</li>\n<li>\n<div>Develop a differential (reflux vs malignancy)</div>\n</li>\n</ul>\n</li>\n<li>\n<div>You perform <strong>flexible laryngoscopy</strong> (minor procedure)</div>\n</li>\n</ul>\n<h3>Billing</h3>\n<ul>\n<li>\n<div><strong>Office visit (9920x/9921x) + modifier 25</strong></div>\n</li>\n<li>\n<div><strong>Flexible laryngoscopy CPT</strong></div>\n</li>\n</ul>\n<div>Again: the E/M is <strong>separate and significant</strong>, not just “scope-only.”</div>"
      },
```

### Note 1768684193385
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "after": {
        "name": "Extra",
        "value": "Memory hook: “conus = cricoid.”<br><img alt=\"Vocal cords: structure and function | Kenhub\" src=\"FLsLvOTe0T7NZMALGN84w_Conus_elasticus_3.png\"><div>Quadrangular Membrane (Supraglottis)</div><div><br></div><div>Located above the ventricle.</div><div><br></div><div>Attachments:</div><div><br></div><div>* Superior: lateral epiglottis</div><div>* Posterior: arytenoid cartilage</div><div><br></div><div>Free edges:</div><div><br></div><div>* Superior free edge → aryepiglottic fold</div><div>* Inferior free edge → vestibular ligament (forms the false vocal fold)</div><div><br></div><div>Function:</div><div><br></div><div>* Supports the supraglottic larynx</div><div>* Forms the framework of the false cords and aryepiglottic folds</div><div><br></div><div>Conus Elasticus (Glottis/Subglottis)</div><div><br></div><div>Located below the ventricle.</div><div><br></div><div>Attachments:</div><div><br></div><div>* Inferiorly: superior border of the cricoid cartilage</div><div>* Anteriorly: thyroid cartilage</div><div>* Posteriorly: vocal process of the arytenoid</div><div><br></div><div>Free superior edge:</div><div><br></div><div>* Vocal ligament (forms the true vocal fold)</div><div><br></div><div>Function:</div><div><br></div><div>* Supports the true vocal folds</div><div>* Forms the cricothyroid membrane anteriorly</div><div>* Provides the elastic recoil of the vocal folds</div>"
      },
      "before": {
        "name": "Extra",
        "value": "Memory hook: “conus = cricoid.”<br><img alt=\"Vocal cords: structure and function | Kenhub\" src=\"FLsLvOTe0T7NZMALGN84w_Conus_elasticus_3.png\">"
      },
      "ordinal": 1
    }
  ],
  "method": "guid",
  "new": "1768684193385",
  "old": "1768684193385",
  "tags_added": [],
  "tags_removed": []
}
```

```json
        "value": "Helps differentiate pediatric vs young adult conductive hearing loss.<br><br><strong>Congenital stapes fixation:</strong> The <strong>stapes superstructure is derived from the 2nd branchial arch (Reichert cartilage)</strong>, while the <strong>footplate has contributions from both the 2nd arch and otic capsule</strong>. ### Note 1769872766701
```
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "after": {
        "name": "Text",
        "value": "The terminal branch of the {{c3::nasociliary}} nerve is the {{c1::anterior ethmoidal}} nerve, which supplies the {{c4::medial nasal ala::location of sensory input}} via the {{c2::external nasal}} nerve."
      },
      "before": {
        "name": "Text",
        "value": "The terminal branch of the {{c3::nasociliary}} nerve is the {{c1::anterior ethmoidal}} nerve, which supplies the medial nasal ala via the {{c2::external nasal}} nerve."
      },
      "ordinal": 0
    },
    {
      "after": {
        "name": "Extra",
        "value": "CN V → V1 (ophthalmic) → nasociliary nerve → anterior ethmoidal nerve → external nasal nerve.<br>Medial nasal ala sensation is V1-derived, an important distinction from lateral nasal ala sensation.<br>If you want to numb up the nose in an awake patient, do a V2 block at the infraorbital foramen and an injection of the lateral sidewall of the nose near the junction o fthe nasal bones and the upper lateral cartilages<br><img alt=\"Anterior ethmoidal nerve - e-Anatomy - IMAIOS\" src=\"8b05846ec6b94a56a674938b7ea6e96ad00ea73f2ada64e0db42ecd1727c4595.jpg\"><br><img alt=\"Surgical Anatomy of the External and Internal Nose | Springer Nature Link\" src=\"https://media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/chp%3A10.1007%2F978-3-030-21217-9_4/MediaObjects/370103_1_En_4_Fig5_HTML.jpg\"><br><img alt=\"Nasociliary nerve | Radiology Reference Article | Radiopaedia.org\" src=\"ce724ed57bc04226cdaca42f25434f_big_gallery.jpeg\"><br><br>"
      },
      "before": {
        "name": "Extra",
        "value": "Medial nasal ala sensation is V1-derived, an important distinction from lateral nasal ala sensation.<br><img alt=\"Anterior ethmoidal nerve - e-Anatomy - IMAIOS\" src=\"8b05846ec6b94a56a674938b7ea6e96ad00ea73f2ada64e0db42ecd1727c4595.jpg\"><br><img alt=\"Surgical Anatomy of the External and Internal Nose | Springer Nature Link\" src=\"https://media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/chp%3A10.1007%2F978-3-030-21217-9_4/MediaObjects/370103_1_En_4_Fig5_HTML.jpg\"><br><img alt=\"Nasociliary nerve | Radiology Reference Article | Radiopaedia.org\" src=\"ce724ed57bc04226cdaca42f25434f_big_gallery.jpeg\"><br><br>"
      },
      "ordinal": 1
    }
  ],
  "method": "guid",
  "new": "1769872766701",
  "old": "1769872766701",
  "tags_added": [],
  "tags_removed": []
}
```

### Note 1771547377514
```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "after": {
        "name": "Text",
        "value": "Surgical repair of SSCD has an overall success rate of {{c1::~94%::percent}}, with no clear difference between {{c2::middle cranial fossa and transmastoid::2}} approaches."
      },
      "before": {
        "name": "Text",
        "value": "Surgical repair of SSCD has a success rate of {{c1::>90%::percent}}, regardless of surgical approach."
      },
      "ordinal": 0
    },
    {
      "after": {
        "name": "Extra",
        "value": "<div>Repair techniques include plugging, resurfacing, capping, and plugging + resurfacing. Older pooled data found ~94% overall success without a statistically significant difference among techniques, although the literature is heterogeneous and individual studies have suggested less durable results with resurfacing alone.&nbsp;</div><div><a href=\"https://otosurgeryatlas.stanford.edu/otologic-surgery-atlas/vestibular-surgery/superior-semicircular-canal-dehiscence/#\">https://otosurgeryatlas.stanford.edu/otologic-surgery-atlas/vestibular-surgery/superior-semicircular-canal-dehiscence/#</a></div><div><br></div><div><img src=\"paste-99b309d8d0b7e0426161c6705e3a4a1a9c6fdf58.jpg\"></div><div><img src=\"paste-5643bad4c4bc32740a17c717bb85ed1af0d31f04.jpg\"></div> <div></div>"
      },
      "before": {
        "name": "Extra",
        "value": "Meta-analyses show overall success ~94% with no statistically significant differences between middle fossa and transmastoid approaches."
      },
      "ordinal": 1
    }
  ],
  "method": "guid",
  "new": "1771547377514",
  "old": "1771547377514",
  "tags_added": [],
  "tags_removed": []
}
```
